### INFERRED

- Ticket 01 is in implementation and verification, not hand-off. The evidence
  is the running worker state, dirty tree, no commits ahead of integration, and
  recent red-green test activity.
- The initial reading/setup phase for worker 01 appears to have run from
  roughly 08:05:34 through 08:08:34, based on selected transcript commands
  reading memory, skills, repo instructions, the spec, decisions, ticket 01,
  surrounding harness/toolbox code, and the repository gate. Implementation and
  test cycles began by 08:10. This is approximate because I am not using private
  reasoning content.
- The baseline is earlier than the worker process launch for ticket 01, which
  improves on the Sureal late-baseline failure. It is still not the recursive
  external-state digest required by stories 34 and 35; it records main/worktree
  heads, dirty state and disk, not protected entries by kind, size, mtime and
  digest.
- The claim commit `3a476b0` moved the integration branch between the baseline's
  recorded integration tip `c9a4bd9` and the worker launch base `3a476b0`. That
  is not a stale-base problem for worker 01, because the launch record uses
  `3a476b0`; it does show why later lead commits on the same integration branch
  can invalidate running worker Candidates.
- The monitor columns are useful for high-level state, but they do not show the
  actual changed paths or the red-green test timeline. I had to inspect the
  worktree and selected transcript records to recover those.
- No urgent attention item is warranted in this pass. I saw no lead write into
  the worker worktree, no review/landing without a Candidate, no parallel worker
  collision, and no worker move of a shared branch.

### DESIGN

- Keep tracker and lead bookkeeping off the Candidate landing branch, or make
  landing explicitly tolerate lead-only tracker commits. The lead already
  recorded the stale-Candidate risk before the first worker finished.
- The marker-commit hand-off rule is the right thing to watch closely. It should
  remove the done-before-commit race if the worker remembers to make the marker
  and stops writing after it; this run has not reached that point yet.
- The first-level design needs a durable, redacted action summary for each
  worker. Today, the public monitor says "running" and dirty count; the useful
  process timeline came from manual transcript selection.
- The observer/ticker is itself a live-run intervention. If periodic observation
  is part of making I1 reliable, controllers should eventually own timer-driven
  observation and transcript discovery instead of relying on an agent patching
  scratch tooling mid-run.
- Automated launch baselines are already valuable, but stories 34 and 35 need a
  stronger protected-state model than the current scratch baseline.

- Verification progress should be first-class in the run record. A lead should
  not need transcript inspection to know which required checks have passed and
  which check is currently running.

  tooling. It made growth visible during ticket 01 without disturbing the
  worker.

  marker-commit wait path: the lead did not need to type into the worker pane or
  inspect the worker tree to keep the ticket moving.

### INFERRED

- Ticket 01 has been waiting on the required gate for at least the interval
  from 08:21:39Z to 08:33:02Z. The worker has not reached report writing,

- The failing gate appears outside ticket 01's changed Python/doc paths, in the
  native SQLite-controller check script, but the worker has not yet produced a
  final report or decided whether to treat it as an unrelated blocker.
- Disk growth is now substantial for a no-commit worker phase: 214M at 08:18,
  370M at 08:23, 928M at 08:33. The monitor exposed this trend, but there is no
  visible threshold or alert.
- The lead remained passive during the long gate wait. That preserves the
  lead/worker boundary, but it also means the only current signal is "running"
  until the worker itself reports or the command exits.

### DESIGN

- Long required gates need structured progress and failure classification. A
  lead-driven run should distinguish "gate still running", "gate failing in
  touched area", and "gate failing in unrelated prerequisite" without transcript
  spelunking.
- The dirty-count-only monitor is not enough for disk pressure. It should
  expose growth rate or a threshold when worker disk use nearly triples during a
  single verification phase.
- The run needs an explicit policy for out-of-scope required-gate failures:
  whether the worker may hand off with a documented blocker, must rerun a
  narrower gate, or must wait for the whole command to terminate.

### INFERRED

- The original marker-commit rule failed in the exact way the lead protocol was
  meant to test: a ready marker existed at `88baa03` and the watcher woke, but
  the worker then rewrote the branch tip to `c153084`. The final tree may be the
  same, but the reviewed Candidate identity changed.
- The worker's cleanup of duplicate attribution was well-intentioned, but it
  crossed the hand-off stability assumption: once a ready Candidate has been
  observed, later rewrites must be detected and recorded, not silently replaced.
- The first review was substantive and source-based. Passing tests and sampled
  claims were not enough; the blocking findings came from comparing the harness
  behavior against the ticket/spec, mirroring the Sureal case-study lesson.
- `ws.py` needed its second live patch before ticket 01 could safely rework:
  after a review is sent, the same marker commit would otherwise remain
  `ready` and wake the lead again.
- The lead log's newly written entries use future-looking minute labels
  (`08:47`, `08:48`, `08:50`, `08:53`) even though file mtimes and transcript
  records show they were written around `08:45-08:46`. The journal should
  prefer transcript timestamps and file mtimes when exact sequencing matters.

### DESIGN

- Candidate identity must be pinned at first readiness observation. A later
  branch-tip change is a distinct event (`candidate-moved`), even if the tree is
  unchanged.
- Interactive-worker readiness needs all of: marker commit, clean tree, session
  idle, and no prior review sent for that same Candidate. A marker commit alone
  is not enough.
- Rework needs its own state in the coordinator, separate from `ready`; otherwise
  a sent-back Candidate can re-trigger the lead without new worker action.
- Review artifacts worked better as files plus a recorded digest than terminal
  prose alone. The worker read `review-1.md` from the state directory after
  receipt.
- Lead action logs are useful, but manual timestamps can drift. The real system
  should write event times automatically when it records review, send, wait and
  coordinator-change events.

### INFERRED

- Ticket 01 is in review-driven rework and has not produced a new Candidate
  after `review-1.md`. The existing marker commit `c153084` remains the branch
  tip, but uncommitted edits make it no longer a ready hand-off.
- The rework loop is following a useful red-green pattern: the worker observed
  failing lead-driven tests for missing marker participants, then got worker
  tests and the expanded lead-driven test suite green.
- The coordinator's new `rework`/`running` distinction is working at the monitor
  level: after review, the same marker commit no longer keeps the ticket in a
  ready-for-lead state once the worker has dirty rework in progress.
- The lead has not acted in this interval after the review handoff. That is
  appropriate; the worker is actively repairing the reviewed Candidate.

### DESIGN

- Review-driven rework benefits from a monitor state that keeps the old
  Candidate visible while showing that new uncommitted work is underway.
- Marker-mode tests should include both positive readiness and negative race
  cases. The worker's first failing test run shows those cases were not natural
  to the original hand-off design and had to be added explicitly.
- Once a review is sent, the system should track "current reviewed Candidate"
  and "current mutable branch tip" separately. During this tick they are already
  different concepts even though the branch tip had not moved yet.

### INFERRED

- Ticket 01 completed one full lead-driven loop: first implementation, review,
  rework in the same interactive worker session, second review, exact-Candidate
  gate, fast-forward landing, resolution and acceptance record.
- Artifact timestamps give a shorter and more exact phase timeline than the
  lead's manual log labels: launch `08:04:52Z` to first stable Candidate
  `08:42:05Z` was about 37 minutes; review send-back was recorded at
  `08:44:44Z`; rework to the second Candidate at `09:02:39Z` was about 18
  minutes; landing was recorded at `09:05:18Z`; and the resolve/acceptance
  commits landed at `09:05:46Z`.
- The second review was narrow and directly tied to the review delta. The lead
  did not rerun the earlier broken broad gate, and the landing artifact records
  the exact gate used.
- Same-session rework worked once, but it consumed most of the worker's context.
  The next rework round would likely need a new or compacted worker context.
- The monitor made new worker existence and disk use visible, but it did not
  expose behind-count or base skew. Ticket 02 and ticket 08 were already behind
  the integration tip at launch because claims were committed one at a time.
- The lead's own notes now correct an earlier instrumentation problem: manual
  lead-log time labels can run ahead of real timestamps, so the journal should
  continue to prefer transcript times, commit times and state-file times.

### DESIGN

- A land record should name the exact Candidate, previous integration tip,
  command gate and landing time. Ticket 01's `landed.json` is the strongest
  process artifact so far.
- Worker briefs should include the exact gate for the ticket. A generic repo
  gate caused wasted time and was later explicitly forbidden in the revised
  launch prompt.
- "For later tickets" findings need an append-only home outside a rewritten
  ticket report, or the hand-off stream can lose useful observations during
  rework.
- Parallel launch needs batch claim semantics or a tracker state outside the
  code Candidate branch. Otherwise the act of starting worker N+1 makes worker
  N stale before it writes any code.
- Monitors need a `behind` or `base` signal, not only `ahead` and `dirty`.
  "0 ahead, 0 dirty" is not enough to tell whether a worker can land without
  re-integration.
- Lead action logs should be auto-stamped by the tool that records the action;
  manual labels are useful narrative, but not reliable evidence for elapsed
  time.

### INFERRED

- The first parallel worker batch is active but still pre-handoff: no new worker
  has made a commit ahead of its launch base, and all three trees are dirty.
- All three workers are spending the early phase reading the required docs and
  code, then entering test-driven work with initial failing or partially failing
  commands before narrow successes.
- The overlap risk is no longer hypothetical. Even before any Candidate exists,
  all three workers have uncommitted changes to the same lead-driven test file,
  and two workers have uncommitted changes to the same harness implementation.
- The lead has not yet acted on the overlap in this interval, at least not in
  selected transcript records or scratch notes. The workers continue
  independently.
- Monitor output is useful for activity, dirty count and context burn, but file
  collision detection still required direct git status checks in each worktree.

### DESIGN

- First-level lead-driven tooling needs an overlap report while workers are
  running, not only at review or landing time. The minimum useful view is
  "dirty paths by worker" plus shared path names.
- Dirty counts alone are too coarse for a lead managing parallel work. A count
  of `3` can mean independent ticket-local files or a direct collision on a
  common test file.
- The worker monitor's context remaining field is valuable during parallel
  work. It shows workers 02/08/13 burning context quickly during the reading
  and first-test phase.
- Backgrounded test commands in worker sessions should be represented in the
  coordinator state. At this tick, workers 02, 08 and 13 all had recent or
  running test activity, but the main monitor only summarized one current shell
  for some workers.

  `bench/harness/test_lead_driven.py` before ticket 13's reworked Candidate
  became clean. The monitor did not show the shared path names.
- Source: ticket 13 state directory at `2026-10-05T09:25:38Z`. No
  `landed.json` existed for ticket 13 yet; only launch and review files were
  present.

### INFERRED

- Ticket 13 completed a very fast review/rework cycle while tickets 02 and 08
  were still on their first pass: roughly 11 minutes from launch to first
  Candidate, about 2 minutes from first Candidate to review handoff, and about
  5 minutes from review receipt to the second marker Candidate.
- The revised no-rewrite protocol worked for ticket 13's rework: the worker
  added `26652eb` and `fcfd998` on top of the reviewed `37b0f5b` rather than
  replacing it.
- The marker-written-past failure repeated in a weaker form. At the 09:23
  monitor, ticket 13's head was a marker commit but the worker was still active
  with a dirty tree. The coordinator correctly reported `running`, not `ready`,
  until the tree became clean and the pane idle.
- Parallel collision pressure is growing. Tickets 02 and 08 were still editing
  the same harness files that ticket 13 had just moved through review and
  rework. If ticket 13 lands first, those workers will need integration or
  conflict handling against live changes in shared files.
- The lead began the ticket 13 landing gate at 09:25. This pass has evidence of
  the gate starting, but not its result or a landing artifact.

### DESIGN

- The `finishing` rule needs to be preserved in the real workflow. Ticket 13
  again showed that a marker commit can exist while the session is still writing
  follow-up edits.
- Review handoff should include the exact no-rewrite instruction, as in ticket
  13's `review-1.md`; this produced clean additive rework.
- Shared-path collision reporting should be live and proactive. It would have
  shown the lead that ticket 13's reviewed files overlapped with still-running
  tickets 02 and 08 before landing started.
- Fast tickets can overtake slower dependency-adjacent work. Lead-driven
  scheduling needs to distinguish dependency graph readiness from file-level
  collision readiness.

  `09:35:55Z`, failed again at `09:36:11Z`, and passed at `09:36:36Z`.

### INFERRED

- Ticket 13 landed cleanly, and ticket 05 filled the worker slot almost
  immediately after the acceptance/resolve commits.
- Ticket 02 exposed the first real re-integration case. The worker had to
  rebase because ticket 13 moved integration underneath it, and the rebase
  conflicted before succeeding.
- The `finishing` state continues to prevent false readiness. Worker 02 had a
  marker commit and clean tree at `09:33`, but the pane was compacting context;
  by `09:36` the worker had written past that marker with another uncommitted
  edit.
- The ticket 02 review found a defect that tests had missed because the
  acceptance item was too broad. The lead corrected the intended boundary in
  the review: avoid worker-worktree mutation and avoid rebases, but advance a
  checked-out target branch through its own checkout so ref, index and files
  move together.
- Ticket 05's worker spawned a subagent in the same worktree. The selected
  records so far show read-only exploration, but this crosses the simple "one
  worker per ticket, one writer per worktree" mental model and should be
  watched.
- Ticket 08 is close to a handoff from a verification perspective, but still has
  no commits and remains dirty. Its context display jumped upward compared with
  the last pass, consistent with worker compaction/resume.

### DESIGN

- First-level tooling needs an explicit re-integration workflow: detect when the
  integration tip moved, ask or instruct the worker to rebase its own branch,
  capture conflicts, and require a fresh marker commit afterward.
- Acceptance criteria in worker tickets need careful wording. Workers execute
  literal constraints, and an over-broad "do not write worktrees" rule can
  produce a real system bug even when tests pass.
- Readiness monitors should distinguish `marker-clean-but-busy` from ready and
  should preserve that state through model context compaction.
- Subagent use by workers needs a policy. If allowed, the lead needs visibility
  into whether the subagent is read-only or can mutate the same worktree.

  lander and scenario failures were followed by passing targeted suites and a
  growing full gate.
- Ticket 05 has moved quickly from reading into implementation and local
  verification, but remains pre-commit with a broad dirty surface that includes
  tracker, lead-launch, harness and ticket-format contract files.

  and writing its report/marker. It still overlaps with ticket 05 in
  `bench/harness/lead_driven.py` and `bench/harness/test_lead_driven.py`.

  context percentages rose after earlier low-context states. This is useful but
  means "context left" is not a simple monotonic burn-down metric.

### DESIGN

- A clean tree with commits ahead is not enough for readiness; the current
  branch tip must be the marker commit and the pane must be idle. Ticket 02
  illustrates all three axes separately.
- Review/rework state should distinguish "review fixes committed" from
  "ready marker committed". The former is visible in git history but should not
  trigger lead review.
- The system should record when worker context is compacted or resumed, because
  monitor percentages otherwise look like unexplained recovery.
- Scenario tickets continue to collide through the central lead-driven harness.
  The system needs either a scenario registration extension point or explicit
  scheduling around shared harness files.

- Ticket 02 completed the full lead-driven loop. The elapsed phases visible
  from artifacts are roughly: 21m20s launch-to-first-Candidate, 2m21s
  first-Candidate-to-review, 18m39s review-to-final-Candidate, 2m23s
  final-Candidate-to-landing, and 44m43s total launch-to-land.
- The lead appears to have held ticket 08 review until ticket 02 landed, then
  sent the review immediately after ticket 03 was claimed and launched. That
  sequencing avoided forcing ticket 08 to rebase twice for the same integration
  movement.
- Ticket 08 repeated the marker-written-past pattern after review: the worker
  created a post-rebase marker, kept the pane active, and then committed more
  work after the marker. The coordinator showed it as `running`, so the monitor
  did not falsely treat the marker as a stable handoff.
- Worker 05 has reached broad verification but remains pre-handoff and dirty.
  The worker's use of `pkill` is an important boundary observation: ticket
  workers may need to clean up scenario-owned processes, but that authority
  needs to be scoped and visible.
- Ticket 03 started from the exact current integration tip after ticket 02
  landing, which is the first observed launch after the batch-claim fix and
  avoids the earlier launch-time base skew.

### DESIGN

- First-level coordination should record explicit phase timestamps for launch,
  first Candidate, review sent, rebase started/failed/continued, final
  Candidate, and land. Ticket 02's artifact trail is reconstructable, but only
  by combining several sources.
- Reviews need a first-class "held until integration settles" state when the
  lead intentionally delays sending rework to avoid redundant rebases.
- Re-integration should be a visible workflow with conflict status and a
  required fresh stable marker after all post-rebase edits, not just a free-form
  review bullet.
- Worker-owned process cleanup needs a contract: which spawned processes belong
  to the ticket, how they are identified, and how cleanup is reported. Broad
  pattern kills are worth surfacing even when they happen inside the worker's
  own sandbox.

### INFERRED

- Ticket 08 completed its loop in about 57m52s from launch to landing:
  39m31s to first Candidate, about 5m40s from first Candidate to sent review
  after the lead held it, about 10m16s from review read to final marker, and
  about 2m11s from final marker to landing.
- Ticket 05 had a very short first review interval by artifact timestamps:
  first marker at `10:03:12Z`, review sent at `10:05:05Z`. The lead log's
  `~9 min` duration for that review does not match the selected transcript
  window or the review artifact timestamp, so that duration should be treated
  as an estimate rather than an event fact.
- The monitor can show different ticket states within a single observer pass
  because worker commits happen between `status` and `workers` calls. Ticket 05
  moved from dirty/running to clean/finishing between `10:03:05Z` and
  `10:03:11Z`.
- The lead again found a real-layout defect after green worker tests, this time
  in ticket 05's launch command behavior with an integration-branch worktree.
  This continues the pattern that scenario/tool tests alone did not encode the
  lead's actual workspace topology.
- The coordinator script was patched during the run for worker git lock
  handling after ticket 08. This is useful dogfood pressure, but it is also a
  live change to the monitors/launcher while observations are ongoing.
- Ticket 09 started with access to home-level skill and memory files before any
  repo implementation work. For a ticket about external-state reporting, that
  is a concrete example of worker-visible external context that should be
  included in the launch/access profile.

### DESIGN

- The first-level system needs monotonic event records independent of monitor
  polling, especially for fast transitions such as ticket 05 dirty-to-marker
  and ticket 08 ready-to-land-to-next-launch.
- Lead review metadata should use machine timestamps for duration and phase
  accounting. Human-written duration labels are valuable notes, but they should
  not be the source of truth.
- Integration topology should be part of every launch and review gate. A
  "passes in an isolated temp repo" result is not enough if the real lead layout
  keeps the integration branch checked out in its own worktree.
- Worker launch/access profiles should account for built-in skills, memory
  files and other home-directory reads, not just repo paths and explicit
  ticket-owned external directories.

- Ticket 03 reached first Candidate in about 23m59s from launch and received a
  lead review about 2m07s later. The review was triggered by a lead-side
  reproduction of an acceptance-record location/order defect, not by failing
  worker self-tests alone.
- Ticket 03's review protocol intentionally differs from the earlier "commit a
  new marker" pattern: the lead told the worker to commit fixes and stop
  without the marker until the lead sends a re-integration message. That is a
  new handoff sub-state worth modelling explicitly.
- Ticket 05 remains a strong marker-invalidated case. It is clean and has
  review-fix commits, but the branch tip is not a `ready for review` marker and
  the pane is still active, so the monitor keeps it out of `ready`.
- Ticket 09 repeated the early ticket 01 behavior of rewriting a ready marker:
  a marker commit was made and amended within about 13 seconds before the worker
  went idle. The monitor only saw the final clean idle marker state.
- Worker 05's context jumped from `28% left` in the previous tick to about
  `85-86% left` here, another compaction/resume signal rather than linear
  token use.

### DESIGN

- Rework needs explicit sub-states: "fixes requested, no marker expected",
  "reintegrate now", "marker written", and "marker invalidated by later
  commits" are distinct lead decisions even though they can all look like
  `running`.
- The system should capture marker rewrites or amend events as first-class
  handoff evidence. Ticket 09's amend happened before the lead saw the marker,
  but it is still relevant to Candidate identity.
- Lead-side reproduction commands are high-value process evidence. Reviews
  should link the reproduction artifact or command to the review finding so the
  worker sees why a green local suite was insufficient.
- Context reporting needs a compaction marker or generation counter. Ticket 05
  again shows a large upward jump that would otherwise distort wait and capacity
  analysis.

### DESIGN

- The system should represent "review says no marker yet" as an enforceable
  state, or the worker can prematurely signal ready and collapse a staged
  re-integration protocol back into the generic marker path.
- A no-rework success path should still record known limits. Ticket 09 shows the
  lead can accept a Candidate directly while preserving caveats in the
  acceptance record.
- Runtime-support tickets need explicit allowed-read profiles for session logs,
  CLI help and home-level runtime configuration, because workers naturally read
  those while designing parity with another runtime.
- Context displays need to say when they reset due to compaction/resume; without
  that, they cannot be used as a duration or capacity signal.

  with four dirty files at the final snapshot.

### INFERRED

- Ticket 03 is now waiting on the lead's scheduling decision, not on worker
  progress. The lead appears to be intentionally holding its re-integration
  until ticket 05 lands, but the public monitor only shows `ready`.
- Ticket 05 has entered a second re-integration loop caused by ticket 09 landing
  ahead of it. The lead explicitly promised no further landing ahead, but the
  system has no visible reservation or lock for that promise.
- Ticket 05 again demonstrates that a marker subject can coexist with dirty
  files and active work. The monitor classed it as `running`, which is correct,
  but the subject line alone would be misleading.
- Ticket 12's context percentage jumped from `30% left` at the prior final
  snapshot to `95% left` at `10:43:31Z`, then `88% left` at `10:45:03Z`,
  consistent with a worker compaction/resume.
- Ticket 12 is reading private-runtime-adjacent local session structures for a
  runtime feature. The observable commands are legitimate for the ticket, but
  they reinforce that runtime support needs a safe transcript/event API instead
  of ad hoc scraping.

### DESIGN

- "Content accepted, re-integrate only" should be a distinct review artifact
  type and monitor state, separate from a substantive review round. Ticket 05's
  `reintegrate-1.md` is currently stored in `reviews.jsonl`, which blurs that
  distinction.
- If the lead promises that nothing will land ahead of a worker's rebase, the
  coordinator should expose that as a reservation or queue constraint rather
  than a text-only promise in the re-integration message.
- The monitor should expose "ready-subject but dirty" as its own warning state.
  Ticket 05 has now hit this after a second rebase.
- Codex runtime support should prefer structured event summaries over direct
  reads of `.codex` or `.trae` session internals, especially because those logs
  can contain private model reasoning.

  ready-looking subject is invalid when the tree is dirty or the pane is active.
  That should be made explicit in UI/state names.

### INFERRED

- Ticket 05 completed the longest observed ticket so far: about 94 minutes from
  launch at `09:26:14Z` to landing at `11:00:18Z`. The extra cost came mostly
  from real-layout review defects, re-integration, and marker-invalidating
  follow-up work after rebases.
- Ticket 03's held re-integration began immediately after ticket 05 landed and
  immediately hit a conflict. The worker recovered to a clean rebased marker
  within about four minutes, but was still spending verification time at the
  final snapshot.
- Ticket 11 demonstrates a new launch mode: the lead deliberately skipped the
  pre-launch claim commit while another worker was mid-rebase. That avoided
  moving integration under ticket 03 again, but it means the tracker shows
  ticket 11 as `open` while a worker is actively running.
- Ticket 12 is in a liminal post-review state: it has committed review fixes and
  is clean/idle, but the monitor cannot show whether it is waiting for a lead
  re-integration instruction, expected to create a marker, or simply idle.
- The 11:03 monitor views disagreed on ticket 03 ahead/dirty counts while the
  rebase was in progress. That may be ordinary sampling skew between commands,
  but it matters because the lead is making sequencing decisions from these
  displays.

### DESIGN

- The coordinator needs an explicit "active but unclaimed" or "launch without
  claim" state if it will allow workers to start without moving the integration
  branch first. Otherwise tracker truth and worker reality intentionally diverge.
- A post-review state machine should distinguish `fixing`, `fixes committed
  without marker`, `reintegrating`, `verifying rebased marker`, and
  `ready-for-lead`. Ticket 12 and ticket 03 are now both hard to describe with
  the current coarse labels.
- Monitor output should be sampled atomically, or display a timestamp per row,
  when rebases are in progress. The ticket 03 `status` versus `workers`
  mismatch is a small example of why.
- The successful ticket 05 landing shows the exact-candidate lander is doing
  useful work, but the surrounding process still needs a queue/reservation model
  so one ticket's acceptance does not force another worker into repeated rebases.

### INFERRED

- Ticket 03 moved from held re-integration to landed in about 15 minutes after
  the lead sent the re-integration message. The worker's external `/tmp` inode
  failure added a short detour, and the successful workaround used a worktree
  local temp root.
- The lead restored tracker truth for ticket 11 only after ticket 03 landed:
  ticket 11 ran for about 15 minutes before the integration branch recorded
  `Claim tickets 11 for workers`.
- Ticket 12 followed the review protocol better than ticket 03 did earlier: it
  stopped after the non-marker fix commit, waited for re-integration
  permission, then rebased. That made the lead's "do not make the marker yet"
  instruction observable in the record.
- Ticket 11's review shows a live-design feedback loop: the original ticket
  only described ticket-file handoffs, but the run had already developed worker
  report and lead-file handoff practices that became new acceptance
  requirements.
- Multiple identical marker subjects now exist in ticket 03's landed history.
  The exact Candidate hash, not the subject text, is the meaningful hand-off
  identity.
- Ticket 04 is open but deliberately not launched while ticket 12 is editing
  launcher/runtime paths, according to the lead log. This is another informal
  scheduling/overlap decision not represented in the monitor beyond absence of
  a worker.

### DESIGN

- The first-level design needs a durable place for lead-discovered future-ticket
  findings outside mutable worker reports. Ticket 11's review is the clearest
  evidence so far that "handoffs into briefs" must ingest worker reports,
  ticket comments and a lead-owned handoff file together.
- Launching without a claim commit reduced immediate rebase churn, but it
  created a temporary open/running contradiction. If this becomes a supported
  mode, the coordinator should expose "running, claim deferred behind ticket X"
  explicitly.
- Scenario and gate runners should control temp roots or preflight temp-space
  health. Ticket 03's `/tmp` inode exhaustion was an external-state failure that
  a worker could work around, but it was unrelated to ticket behavior.
- Worker-facing readiness should not rely on marker subject text. Rebase,
  amend, hooks and `commit-tree` can all produce multiple ready-subject commits;
  the protocol needs exact Candidate IDs and a stable "final after idle/clean"
  signal.

  land, lead-launch and harness commands were still running in selected output
  at `11:27:37Z`, `11:29:12Z`, `11:31:42Z`, and `11:34:04Z`. The worker
  inspected integration/landing code and diffs, and the final status showed
  `10` dirty paths on top of `8396875`.

### INFERRED

- Ticket 12 completed the deferred re-integration handoff correctly: it waited
  for the lead's message, rebased, reran checks, then produced a new clean
  `ready for review` Candidate about 16 minutes after permission.
- Ticket 11 also obeyed the "fix and stop, no new marker until re-integrate"
  protocol. The monitor still reported it as generic `running`, even though the
  pane and commit history show a clean, idle, intentional waiting state.
- The `/tmp` external-state problem seen on ticket 03 repeated on ticket 12's
  scenario gate. Worktree-local `TMPDIR` is becoming a practical run invariant,
  not a one-off workaround.
- Ticket 11's context percentage jumped from `27% left` at `11:23` to `78%`
  at `11:33`, and ticket 12 jumped from `29%` to `80%`, consistent with
  compaction/resume rather than linear budget use.
- The lead started acting on ticket 12 readiness almost immediately after the
  marker became visible, but the artifact trail had not yet caught up by this
  pass. This is a short wait where the lead is busy, not where a worker is
  waiting unseen.

### DESIGN

- The monitor needs a state for "review fixes committed; waiting for lead
  re-integration instruction" distinct from both `running` and `ready`. Ticket
  11 is the cleanest example because the pane explicitly recorded the reason.
- Temp-root selection should be part of the harness contract. Two workers have
  now had scenario loops fail under default temp placement and pass under
  worktree `.scratch`.
- Lead review/landing in progress should have an artifact or monitor flag before
  the landing result exists. At `11:33`, the only evidence was the lead
  transcript, so observers and workers cannot see that the lead has already
  started on ticket 12.
- Context accounting should treat compaction/resume as a visible event. The
  raw percentage alone is misleading for long-running worker comparisons.

- Ticket 12 became the first ticket landed through the effort's own durable
  acceptance and landing tools instead of only the scratch coordinator's
  `ws.py land` path. That path left different artifacts: durable
  `acceptance-12.jsonl` and acceptance notes, but no scratch `landed.json`.
- Ticket 12's full span was about 70 minutes from launch (`10:25:31Z`) to
  landing (`11:35` lead action), including one review round and one
  re-integration. The lead explicitly did not verify real resume behavior.
- Ticket 04 was launched immediately after ticket 12 landed, and ticket 11 was
  then told to re-integrate. This matches the lead log's earlier decision to
  hold ticket 04 while ticket 12 owned the launcher/runtime area.
- Ticket 11 again paid a rebase conflict after integration moved. It recovered
  quickly, reran broad checks and became ready roughly eight minutes after
  receiving the re-integration message.
- Ticket 07 became ready just after the 11:43 monitor snapshot. This creates a
  common observer race: monitor state said dirty/running, while transcript and
  final pane/head checks a minute later showed clean/ready.
- Ticket 04's initial read phase followed the amended ticket content from the
  live run, including the two worker-session kinds and "fixes committed,
  waiting for re-integration" state.

### DESIGN

- If two landing paths are supported, their artifact contract should be the
  same. Ticket 12's durable acceptance record is useful, but the missing scratch
  `landed.json` means tooling that watches only coordinator cache misses a
  landing.
- The monitor needs a way to mark "lead review/landing in progress" and to tie
  it to an eventual artifact. The 11:33-11:35 ticket 12 interval was visible
  only by reading the lead transcript until integration advanced.
- Re-integration conflict handling is now a normal path, not an exception:
  tickets 02, 03, 05, 08, 11 and 12 have all hit or worked around stale-base
  pressure in some form.
- Ticket 16 captures a structural answer emerging from the run: bookkeeping
  commits are making code Candidates stale. The readiness design should treat
  bookkeeping storage as a first-level concern, not a later polish item.

### INFERRED

- Ticket 11 took about 48 minutes from launch (`11:00:44Z`) to integration
  landing (`11:48:15Z`), with one review, one deferred re-integration and a
  final landing through the durable acceptance plus toolbox lander path.
- The monitor correctly showed ticket 11 landed after integration advanced, but
  the scratch cache again lacked a per-ticket `landed.json` because the lead did
  not use `ws.py land`.
- The lead held ticket 06 even though a worker slot was free because amending
  ticket 06 and claiming it would add integration commits while ticket 07 was
  already being asked to rebase. This is another example of bookkeeping commits
  consuming coordination capacity.
- Ticket 07's review found the same real-layout class already seen in tickets
  02, 03 and 05: a tool passed one-checkout tests but failed when run from the
  main checkout against an effort record that exists only in the integration
  worktree.
- Ticket 04 discovered another scenario-output path sharp edge: relative
  `--out` paths behave differently once the harness changes working directory
  into worker worktrees.
- The lead's `ws.py` change turned a repeated review finding into standing
  launch-brief guidance, which is a useful dogfooding feedback loop even though
  it happened in scratch first.

### DESIGN

- The first-level design needs a first-class model for repository topology.
  "Main checkout, integration worktree, worker worktrees" is not an incidental
  implementation detail; tests, briefs and tool APIs need to name it directly.
- Landing metadata should be written through a single durable path or mirrored
  between durable evidence and coordinator cache. Ticket 11 repeats the ticket
  12 artifact split.
- The launcher should make idle-slot reasons visible. At this tick, capacity
  existed, but the slot was intentionally idle because a ticket amendment and
  claim would disturb ticket 07's re-integration.
- Scenario runners should normalize or reject relative output directories at
  the boundary, because workers naturally use `.scratch` paths and the harness
  executes from nested checkouts.

  reproduced two-worktree failure into focused tests and fixes, but its broad
  gate was still running at the cutoff.
- Both active workers are losing context quickly: ticket 04 dropped from 33% at
  the prior cursor to 17% by `12:03`, while ticket 07 dropped from 49% to 42%.
  The remaining phases are verification, report writing and final commit, so
  late compaction risk is rising for ticket 04 in particular.
- Ticket 04's relative `--out` failure and ticket 07's scenario `mainline`
  correction both show that lead-driven scenario contracts are now influencing
  worker behavior as much as production tool code.

### DESIGN

- Monitor rows should distinguish "dirty after a passing gate" from ordinary
  dirty implementation. Ticket 04 had passed broad checks, then made a cleanup
  edit and therefore returned to an uncertain state.
- Worker context left should probably be part of launch/review decisions. At
  17% context, ticket 04 is in the highest-risk phase for losing its final
  report/commit trail.
- Scenario definitions need explicit validation for branch and output-path
  assumptions. Ticket 07's `mainline = "integration"` and ticket 04's relative
  `.scratch` output both produced misleading failures that were not core ticket
  defects.

### INFERRED

- Ticket 07 took about 59 minutes from launch (`11:15:56Z`) to landing
  (`12:14-12:15`), including one review and one rebase/rework pass.
- The lead's real-repo check found a regression within minutes of landing
  ticket 07. The cause was the lead's own review requirement: the new
  multi-worktree lookup treated old effort directories copied across many
  worktrees as ambiguous.
- Ticket 16's scope changed from the planned bookkeeping branch question to an
  immediate repair of just-landed effort-record lookup behavior. The lead
  chose this because ticket 16 already owned bookkeeping location and touched
  the same tool set.
- Ticket 04 continued making progress after compaction and was in the final
  verification/report phase, but it remained uncommitted. Its elapsed first
  pass was about 41 minutes by the final monitor.
- The monitor's wait display still includes resolved tickets in ticket 14's
  waits. That may be a static dependency list rather than a live blocker list,
  but it is confusing once several dependencies are already landed.

### DESIGN

- Review requirements need their own post-landing validation on the real
  repository topology before being generalized into shared helper behavior.
  Ticket 07 passed the targeted reproduction but still broke older efforts.
- A lead-side "tell/note" primitive emerged because the run needed to pass a
  discovered integration defect to an already-launched worker without a full
  review cycle. That is distinct from review feedback and should be a named
  first-level message type if retained.
- Static wait lists and live launchability should be separated in monitor
  output. Ticket 14's row kept resolved dependencies visible, while the run
  had already acted on several of them.
- Context percentage without compaction history is misleading. Ticket 04 looked
  low-risk again after compaction, but it had already spent a long first pass
  and still had no Candidate.

### INFERRED

- Ticket 04's first Candidate took about 44 minutes from launch to review
  handoff (`11:36:03Z` to `12:20:06Z`) and only about 2 minutes from handoff to
  lead review send. The worker did not remain idle after signalling ready; it
  resumed immediately when the review arrived.
- The re-integration tax repeated on ticket 04. Even though it was launched
  after ticket 07 landed, the ticket 04 branch still had to rebase over the
  later claim/amend commits for tickets 06 and 16, and that rebase produced a
  test conflict.
- Ticket 06 is implementing the explicit overlap surfacing that the observer
  had been recording manually: declared-path overlap at launch, active changed
  paths, and behind-integration status. This is a direct design response to the
  earlier blind spots in the monitor.
- Ticket 16 is treating the ticket 07 post-landing regression as part of the
  bookkeeping boundary. Its first red tests make the desired durable record
  branch behavior visible before production changes.
- The lead's own log usefully preserves a review-confidence distinction: one
  ticket 04 finding was not reproduced, but followed from reading the code
  against the newly landed ticket 07 lookup behavior.

### DESIGN

- "Ready" should probably be split into "candidate available" and "lead review
  completed or deferred"; ticket 04 was ready/clean for only a short window
  before it became dirty rework again.
- Lead review records should mark each finding's evidence type: reproduced
  failure, static code inspection, or process requirement. The ticket 04 review
  shows why that distinction matters.
- The first-level monitor needs built-in overlap and behind-tip reporting.
  Ticket 06 is now encoding precisely the manual checks the observer and lead
  had to perform during earlier parallel tickets.
- Moving bookkeeping off the code branch needs compatibility for legacy
  effort directories that do not yet have an effort record. Ticket 16's local
  fallback is a concrete response to the real-repo failure discovered after
  ticket 07 landed.

- The lead is idle or waiting during this interval after sending ticket 04
  review-1; all visible movement is worker-side implementation and rework.
- Ticket 04 is no longer in a stable ready state despite the head subject and

  the rebased ready marker, so a later final marker will be needed.
- Tickets 04 and 06 are actively colliding on `tools/agents/worker.py` and its
  tests, while ticket 06 and 16 both touch `lead_launch.py`. Ticket 06's own
  feature is being built while the run is producing the kind of overlap it is
  meant to expose.
- Ticket 16 has converted the post-07 real-repo regression into concrete red
  tests, then made progress until effort/tracker/acceptance tests were green;
  landing and launch paths remain unresolved at the cutoff.

  and ticket 16 to 38%, all while still dirty and pre-handoff.

### DESIGN

- Head subject and ahead count are not sufficient worker-state signals. Ticket
  04 shows why monitors need a "dirty after ready marker" or "review rework in
  progress" state that overrides the marker subject.
- Active overlap should be computed from dirty paths, not just declared ticket
  paths. At this tick the real hot files are `worker.py`, `test_worker.py` and
  `lead_launch.py`.
- The overlap tooling being implemented by ticket 06 should probably observe
  both launch-time declaration overlap and live dirty-path overlap. The live
  run is already demonstrating both surfaces.
- Long-running fake-process test suites dominate feedback time for workers 04
  and 06. First-level orchestration may need to distinguish "waiting on local
  tests" from "waiting on another agent" in the monitor.

### INFERRED

- The lead spent this interval waiting after the ticket 04 review; no lead-side
  tool action or integration movement was visible.
- Ticket 04 likely compacted or otherwise reset context again: the monitor

  review-rework thread continued. Its dirty state and active shell mean the

- The live run now has a three-way shared-file collision: tickets 04, 06 and
  16 all touch `bench/harness/lead_driven.py` and

  `worker.py`; tickets 04, 06 and 16 touch `lead_launch.py`.

- Ticket 16 moved most narrow tool tests green and added policy-versioned docs
  for a record branch, but its broad lead-driven suite was still red during
  the observed window.

### DESIGN

- The first-level design needs live collision reporting before workers reach
  the commit/rebase phase. By the time ticket 06's feature is ready, several
  existing workers may already have paid for the lack of that signal.
- Context percentages should be paired with a compaction/resume event count.
  Ticket 04's apparent recovery to 66% hides that it is a long-running ticket
  in its second rework segment.
- Policy/doc version bumps can become part of ordinary ticket work. The
  workflow should make clear when a worker is allowed to update canonical
  policy files versus only per-effort evidence and tools.
- The monitor should say "running local tests" with command age when a worker
  is blocked on long fake-process suites; otherwise the status looks like
  generic agent deliberation.

  `10-watching-limits-accounting` worker window.

### INFERRED

- Ticket 04 finished a complete rework-loop handoff and landing, but the lead
  still had to know which checkout could see the effort record. The first two
  failed landing attempts are another example of the three-checkout problem
  leaking into lead procedure.
- Moving ticket 04 before tickets 06 and 16 finished caused the expected
  re-integration pressure: ticket 16 was explicitly told to rebase in review-1,
  and ticket 06's review told it not to create another marker until ticket 16
  lands.
- Ticket 06 produced the clearest runtime-readiness blocker so far. Fake-runner
  tests can pass while a real sandboxed worker in a git worktree cannot commit
  because the worktree's mutable git metadata is outside the worker's writable
  root.
- Ticket 16's central record-branch idea worked in the lead's temporary
  reproduction, but the accepted-worker-report path is now part of the same
  state model. Without it, future-ticket findings, claim sampling and
  acceptance evidence split across the code and record branches.
- The monitor can lag direct git state during fast review/rework cycles. In
  this pass it reported ticket 16 clean while a direct status moments later
  showed a dirty harness test after the new marker.

### DESIGN

- First-level collaboration needs an explicit "working directory authority"
  rule for lead tools: a command should either resolve the effort from any
  checkout or fail before doing partial work. The lead should not need to
  remember which checkout owns which operation.
- The runtime adapter contract should normalize tool edit events, not only
  shell commands, if the system will judge whether file changes are accounted
  for.
- The default sandbox/worktree combination needs a preflight that proves a
  worker can stage and commit before a live trial starts. This is a design
  blocker for ticket 14 unless the writable boundary includes the worktree git
  metadata or workers use a different isolation layout.
- Record branches need an accepted-report copy rule as much as they need ticket
  and acceptance records; otherwise the system moves bookkeeping off the code
  branch but loses the report data later briefs depend on.
- The monitor should show "review sent, rework active" and "post-marker dirty"
  as separate states, and its dirty count should be sampled close enough to the
  status decision to avoid stale clean/dirty displays during fast-moving
  rework.

  review-1 at `13:07:03Z`.

### INFERRED

- The lead did not act during this interval after sending ticket 06 review-1;
  all visible progress was worker-side.
- Ticket 06 is in the "fixes committed, wait for re-integration" lane in
  substance, even before the commit is visible: its review explicitly forbids a
  fresh marker until ticket 16 lands, and its pane showed preparation for a
  non-marker review-fix commit.
- Ticket 10 is following a standard red-green loop and had recovered its
  focused and broad lead-driven behavior by the tick, but the broad required
  gate was still running and the tree had no hand-off commit.
- Ticket 16 shows the hardest state-model churn in this interval: a post-review
  marker exists, but the worker immediately wrote past it while making the
  accepted-report/record-branch readers agree.
- The monitor's `last commit / waits` column is now ambiguous for ticket 06 and
  ticket 16. Both show a "ready for review" head subject while the actual state
  is dirty, active rework.

### DESIGN

- "Ready-marker subject plus dirty tree" needs its own visible state, especially
  after review. In this tick both ticket 06 and ticket 16 had ready-marker
  subjects that were no longer stable handoffs.
- Review protocols need to override generic worker wrapper text. Ticket 06's
  wrapper told the worker to finish with a marker, while the review body told
  it to stop without one until re-integration; the worker noticed and followed
  the narrower review instruction.
- Ticket 10's limit accounting tests show why the harness needs deterministic
  knobs for time-based cases. Wall-clock and quiet-time behavior can look red
  because scenario fixture thresholds do not force the intended condition.
- The record-branch design is pulling tracker, acceptance, lead-launch and
  harness semantics together. That argues for one shared record-location API
  rather than independent readers in each tool.

### INFERRED

- The lead remained idle or waiting during this interval; the only observed
  changes were worker-side.
- Ticket 06 is now the clearest example of a clean, idle, reviewed worker that
  is intentionally not ready to land: the head is a review-fix commit, not a
  marker, and the review requires a later re-integration message.
- Ticket 10's context percentage jumped from 21% at `13:23` to 61% at `13:33`,
  implying a compaction/resume while the same ticket continued. Its status text
  mentioned memory citations, which is process noise for a ticket whose product
  is tool/harness behavior.
- Ticket 16 appears to have fixed the immediate accepted-report failures but
  has widened again into policy and manifest changes. That keeps it in the
  high-risk lane even though the latest narrow tests were green.
- The monitor still has no label for "clean/idle after review fix, waiting for
  lead permission", so it reports ticket 06 as generic running.

### DESIGN

- The first-level workflow needs an explicit state for "fix committed, waiting
  for re-integration permission" that does not depend on a marker commit and
  does not look like active implementation.
- Worker prompts should not include a generic final-marker instruction that can
  contradict a lead review. Review handoffs need to be the last word on the
  next hand-off shape.
- Context meters should record compaction/resume events; otherwise a jump from
  21% to 61% looks like capacity recovery rather than context replacement.
- Policy and manifest edits from a worker should be called out by the monitor
  or review queue, because those files carry repo-wide process authority and
  are easy to miss inside a broad dirty count.

### INFERRED

- Ticket 16 went from ready to landed in about five minutes after the prior
  sample, and the lead immediately used the freed slot for ticket 17.
- Ticket 10's worker completed its side of the hand-off, but the candidate was

  ticket 17 claim commits had already moved the integration branch.
- Ticket 06's reintegration is an example of the collision cost becoming real:
  rebasing across ticket 16's record-branch changes caused conflicts in the
  shared launcher and harness code and consumed at least ten minutes before the
  broader gate even finished.
- The lead's ticket 10 note is a concrete requirements-management failure:
  worker behavior followed acceptance checkboxes, while a prose amendment was
  easy to miss and required a new ticket.
- Ticket 17 is now exercising the sandboxed-worker commit blocker directly. It
  reached an early green test result, then went red again after production
  changes, which is normal active implementation rather than hand-off readiness.

  06's rebase: a single status table showed conflict-era dirtiness, while the
  worker detail and direct git status shortly after showed a clean rebased
  branch.

### DESIGN

- Ticket amendments need to update acceptance criteria, not only prose
  descriptions; lead-driven workers optimize for the checklist they are handed.
- The lead queue should separate "ready but stale" from "ready to review or
  land". Ticket 10 is clean at a marker, but it is already behind seven
  integration commits and cannot be landed without reintegration.
- Landing artifacts should have one obvious durable home. Ticket 16 is landed
  according to the branch and status monitor, but its coordinator state
  directory has no per-ticket landed record.
- Monitors should expose transient rebase/conflict state and sample time, since
  the ticket 06 status changed materially within ten seconds.
- Shared-file collision planning needs to consider queued reintegrations, not
  just concurrent dirty work: ticket 06's collision surfaced only when it was
  allowed to rebase after ticket 16 landed.

  ticket 06.

  early green `test_worker.py` and later harness failures show that the core
  product-path scenario still matters.

- Context gauges need an explicit "session compacted/resumed" event; otherwise
  apparent context recovery can be mistaken for a worker becoming less risky.
- The lead-driven state model needs a durable "held ready, stale behind
  integration" state for tickets like 10.
- Command supervision should assign stable command/run IDs visible in the
  monitor so repeated or overlapping gate loops can be disambiguated.
- Worker change-scope monitors should flag when a ticket expands into durable
  process docs or skill files, not only count dirty paths.

### DESIGN

- Ready-state detection needs to update promptly enough that a lead looking at
  the queue sees new candidates within the same minute, or the monitor should
  display its sampling time per worker.

  queue item; otherwise observers see a tool call start with no visible
  artifact until the review finishes.

  has now waited through at least two ticks after its marker because landing
  order is constrained by ticket 06.
- Workers can finish just after a monitor sample, so readiness alerts should be
  event-driven rather than only table polling.

  needs provider-specific acceptance probes for sandbox and git behavior.
- Re-integration requests should include expected conflict hotspots and maybe a
  merge-base report. Ticket 10 immediately collided in the known shared
  launcher/test files.

  provider, verdict, candidate commit and event-stream path or checksum.

  later after reading a handoff. Marker state should be attached to a reviewed
  candidate hash, not inferred from the current head subject alone.
- Real runtime probes should be part of the regular acceptance path for
  infrastructure tickets; they found and then confirmed the central ticket 17
  behavior in one pass.

### DESIGN

- The readiness monitor should separate "dirty after marker due to lead
  re-integration" from ordinary implementation work, especially when the head
  subject still says `ready for review`.
- Host temp/socket constraints are now a repeated test-gate concern. Gate
  runners should set workspace-local `TMPDIR`/`TMUX_TMPDIR` or preflight the
  default temp location before declaring infrastructure tests flaky.
- Context gauges need a compaction/resume indicator; otherwise a worker can
  appear to recover from 22% to 85% context without any visible explanation.
- Untracked generated files like `.local` should be classified separately in
  monitor output from ticket-owned untracked evidence files.

  and tried several private `/tmp` reproductions. The first bwrap reproduction

  still failed at `15:10:56Z` and `15:11:03Z`; pre-creating
  `/tmp/tmux-$(id -u)` with mode `700` made `test_sqlite_baseline.py` pass at

### INFERRED

- Ticket 17 is not blocked on a merge conflict; it is blocked on making the

  `/tmp` permissions and the `/tmp/tmux-$UID` parent directory.

  tmux did not create the per-user socket parent under the bwrap-mounted temp

  remained cleanly rebased onto integration, reinforcing that the marker subject
  alone cannot mean "ready".

  handoff.

### DESIGN

- Gate runners that exercise tmux inside a capsule should provision a normal
  private `/tmp`: mode `1777`, plus the expected `tmux-$UID` socket directory
  when tmux on the host requires it.
- Monitor output should expose whether a dirty tree is post-marker/rework work
  and should show generated scratch paths like `.local` separately.
- Repeated gate runs need stable command IDs or phase names so observers can
  distinguish a failed full-gate attempt, a focused reproduction and a fresh
  full-gate rerun.

### DESIGN

- Re-integration resume needs a first-class contract for what integration ref a
  private worker repository receives before it resumes. Otherwise stale-base
  handling depends on worker/runtime details that are only visible through
  scenario artifacts.
- Candidate publication after resume should be explicit in the lead-driven
  protocol: either resume itself publishes a successor Candidate under a
  declared condition, or the lead/harness must run a separate supervision step
  before landing.
- Low context during active re-integration should be visible to the lead as a
  risk signal, especially when the worker has not yet reached report update or
  marker creation.

  be mistaken for a completed acceptance.

  lead-side gate failed" for a ready ticket; otherwise observers must infer that
  state from the lead transcript and integration worktree.

  `tools/agents/test_lead_launch.py` by `16:04:38Z`. Direct branch status
  showed head `0a28a3dd857524362746fca4e43f677167579c55`, `0` ahead/`0`
  behind, and dirty paths `tools/agents/test_lead_launch.py` and
  `tools/agents/test_worker.py` with 278 insertions.

### INFERRED

- Ticket 17 was landed between the 15:53 and 16:03 monitor samples, but not
  through the same coordinator artifact path as earlier landed tickets: the
  durable branch has acceptance evidence while the cache directory lacks
  `landed.json`.
- The lead's first action after ticket 17 landed was to immediately claim and
  launch ticket 18, but the environment problem had already spread into lead
  operations: even a shell here-document failed because `/tmp` had no free
  inodes.
- Ticket 18 began with useful inherited context. The lead sent the temp-directory
  note before or during the worker's startup, and the worker adopted those temp
  settings before reading and editing.
- The independent integration-branch review is a new actor in the process but
  is not represented in the coordinator's worker table; observers can see it
  only through the lead transcript and scratch log.

### DESIGN

- Landing metadata should be unified: if monitors say a ticket is landed, the
  state directory should either contain `landed.json` or explicitly record which
  alternate landing path supplied the authoritative metadata.
- Environment remediation needs a first-class channel. A lead note to a worker
  helped ticket 18, but the run also needed lead-shell temp handling and a
  record of which commands were run with exceptions to the normal gate.
- Independent reviewers should be modeled explicitly if they are part of the
  lead-driven level. Otherwise they become invisible background work relative
  to the coordinator's ticket/worker model.

### INFERRED

- The run crossed from a ticket-by-ticket readiness effort into a whole-branch
  defect-repair phase. The independent review found several defects that were
  invisible to green per-ticket suites and were immediately converted into
  tickets 19-21.
- The lead acted on the independent review quickly, but the review happened
  late in the run: after ticket 17 had landed and after many tickets had
  already shaped the shared toolbox. That supports the lead's own note that
  independent review should run every few landings, not only near the end.
- Tickets 18, 19 and 20 are now active in parallel and all touch overlapping
  agent-tool files, especially `tools/agents/test_worker.py` and
  `tools/agents/test_lead_launch.py`. The monitor shows dirty counts but not
  the cross-worker path collision.
- Ticket 18 is in implementation and test repair, not close to handoff. Ticket
  19 is still in red-test/scenario setup. Ticket 20 has begun red-test edits
  and runtime-resume command inspection.
- The landed-status mismatch for ticket 17 is now persistent across at least
  one subsequent integration move, not just a transient immediately after
  landing.

### DESIGN

- First-level lead-driven collaboration needs a review cadence for the
  integration branch itself. Per-ticket review is insufficient for defects that
  appear only with simultaneous workers, shared repositories, stale checkouts
  or inconsistent command surfaces.
- The coordinator should surface active path overlap across workers. At this
  tick, three workers are all editing the same small toolbox area, but the
  monitor still exposes only `ahead` and `dirty` counts.
- The task model needs to represent "new defect ticket spawned by independent
  review" as a first-class event, including which findings were assigned and
  which earlier accepted tickets are implicated.
- Lead-side helper changes such as `tell` should become typed protocol
  operations with their own durable records, not ad hoc scratch-script
  features, because they now carry important operational instructions.

  transcripts. Without that, an observer can keep chasing new bytes and blur a
  tick's time boundary.

  `16:47Z`, passed `py_compile` for the harness/scenario files at
  `16:47:32Z`, then saw the focused interactive scenario test fail again at
  `16:48:18Z`.
- Source: direct ticket 19 branch status/diffstat and redacted transcript
  selection from byte offset `7266761` through `9761880`. Ticket 19 stayed at
  head `375c2610ab82cd498e8a18cbf77a0537d2868043`, with twelve tracked dirty
  files plus untracked `bench/readiness/shared-repository-safe/`; tracked
  diffstat was 858 insertions and 126 deletions. A patch to
  `tools/agents/worker.py` failed at `16:35:08Z` and later patches to that
  file succeeded. `python3 -m py_compile` over edited harness and tool files
  exited `0` at `16:42:55Z`. A `python3 tools/agents/test_land.py` command
  exited `1` at `16:43:29Z`. The worker then continued reading diffs and
  land/record-branch code through `16:48Z`, with no commits ahead.
- Source: direct ticket 20 branch status/diffstat and redacted transcript
  selection from byte offset `7389652` through `8598245`. Ticket 20 stayed at
  head `375c2610ab82cd498e8a18cbf77a0537d2868043`, with eight tracked dirty
  files and tracked diffstat 807 insertions and 83 deletions. `py_compile`
  plus focused tool checks exited `0` at `16:37:01Z`; a combined harness suite
  failed at `16:38:31Z`; direct `rework-round` and combined
  `rework-round`/`parallel-workers-and-overlap` scenario commands exited `0`
  by `16:40:44Z`; a broad harness command failed again at `16:42:14Z`;
  `python3 bench/harness/test_lead_driven.py` exited `0` at `16:44:26Z`; the
  broad harness suite exited `0` at `16:46:47Z`; and the all-scenario loop
  exited `0` at `16:48:04Z`. The worker then inspected the ticket and diff at
  `16:48:18Z`, still with no commit ahead.

### INFERRED

- The lead was still waiting on active workers. There was no lead transcript
  growth, no integration movement, no new review artifact and no landing
  artifact during this interval.
- Ticket 18 moved from focused unit recovery into scenario construction, but
  its readiness scenario was still red at the observation cutoff.
- Ticket 19 remained in a broad diagnosis/editing phase. It recovered compile
  checks but still had a failing lander test and the widest shared tooling
  footprint of the three active workers.
- Ticket 20 reached the strongest validation state of the active set: broad
  harness and all-scenario checks passed after earlier failures, but the worker
  had not yet committed or handed off.
- The monitor continues to hide important distinctions: ticket 18's direct
  status included a new untracked scenario directory while the monitor exposed
  only a dirty count, and ticket 19's context jumped from 19% in the earlier
  handoff sample to 93% while the work stayed active and dirty.

### DESIGN

- Monitors should distinguish tracked dirty files, untracked ticket-owned
  scenario/evidence directories and generated artifacts. A single dirty count
  cannot show whether a worker is changing production code, adding a fixture,
  or leaving temp output behind.
- The first-level coordinator needs a current verification field with command
  name and last exit code. Ticket 20 had passed broad gates by the transcript
  cutoff, but the worker row still only showed a generic long-running-shell
  status.
- Context reset/compaction should be a first-class worker event. Without it, a
  19% to 93% jump looks like a measurement error or progress signal instead of
  session lifecycle.
- The process would benefit from an observer or coordinator cutoff primitive:
  while transcripts are live, a tick can otherwise collect events later than
  the nominal `TICK` time.

- Ticket 20 completed its review/landing path during this interval. The lead
  first sent review feedback to the `7538209` candidate, then observed a
  rewritten ready marker `62b50ec`, resolved ticket 20 and recorded acceptance
  before claiming ticket 21.
- The state-cache mismatch repeated for ticket 20: the monitor reports the
  ticket as landed and integration contains the landed commits, but the ticket
  20 state directory listing did not include `landed.json`.
- The integration branch moved under both active tickets 18 and 19. Direct
  branch comparison showed ticket 18 `8` behind and ticket 19 `6` behind, but
  the monitor still showed only `0 ahead` and dirty counts.
- The lead acted on an owner/status concern that workers 18 and 19 might not
  be doing anything by inspecting panes and processes. The record showed both
  were alive; ticket 18 had a long harness path and ticket 19 was in
  integration-failure troubleshooting.
- The coordinator-script change appears to support cleanup/monitoring after
  closing landed worker windows, since landed workers now show no pid/context
  instead of stale live-process details.

### DESIGN

- The first-level monitor needs a behind/rebase-required column now, not only
  `ahead` and `dirty`. Tickets 18 and 19 became stale immediately after ticket
  20 landed and ticket 21 was claimed.
- Landing metadata should be written consistently across landing paths. Ticket
  20 repeats the ticket 17 pattern where the monitor and integration history
  say "landed" while the coordinator state directory lacks `landed.json`.
- Closing landed worker windows is useful cleanup, but it changes historical
  observability: context and pid fields become unknown. If window cleanup is
  part of the workflow, the monitor should preserve a last-seen summary before
  teardown.
- Ticket 21 launch after ticket 20 landing shows the queue can keep throughput
  going, but it also increases rebase pressure on still-running tickets 18 and
  19. The scheduler needs an explicit tradeoff between filling slots and
  protecting long-running dirty work from integration churn.

### INFERRED

- Ticket 18's implementation and handoff phase completed between the 17:13 and
  17:23 ticks; the ready marker was made at `17:22:52Z`, and the lead noticed
  and began review/reproduction within about two minutes.
- Ticket 18's ready branch is already stale relative to integration: it is one
  commit ahead but eight commits behind `effort/lead-worker-readiness`. The
  monitor reported readiness and ahead/dirty state but not the behind count.

  remain hard to compare across compaction/resume phases.
- Worker 21 is in the red/green implementation phase, with failing scenario
  tests observed before the current two-file dirty state.

### DESIGN

- Ready candidates should show both ahead and behind counts, especially before
  lead review begins. Ticket 18 looked clean and ready in the monitor but was
  eight commits behind the integration tip.
- A lead-side review/probe should be a visible phase in the run state. During
  this pass, the lead had started a real validation probe for ticket 18, but
  the monitor still only represented the worker as ready/idle.
- The command recorder or worker brief should make masked pipeline failures
  visible. Ticket 19 had an `rg` error inside a pipeline that returned shell
  exit `0`, which is easy to miss in a transcript-driven workflow.
- The handoff contract may need a clearer "green gates but still not ready"
  state. Ticket 19's broad gate passed, yet the worker continued inspection and
  remained dirty without a candidate.

### INFERRED

  was actively reviewing it with a live reproduction probe, but the worker

  lead-driven harness itself, while remaining within a narrow three-file dirty
  set.

### DESIGN

- Lead review has its own waiting states: waiting on a worker handoff, waiting
  on review reproduction, and waiting on an interactive prompt inside the
  review probe are different bottlenecks and should be visible separately.
- Interactive review probes need an explicit preflight for trust/config prompts
  before they are counted as a meaningful validation run.
- Green gate status should be tied to the exact command set and timestamp. For
  ticket 19, a broad gate passed in one slice and a later broader command failed
  on a specific lead-launch regression within minutes.
- Dirty-count-only monitor fields miss implementation shape. Ticket 21 moving
  from two to three files was more meaningful because the added file was
  `bench/harness/lead_driven.py`, not just because the count increased.

- Ticket 18 entered rework because the lead's real interactive review found

- The ticket 18 worker began rework in the same TraeCLI session and spent the

- Ticket 19 recovered the previously failing lead-launch test and completed a

  differently from clean green gates. Ticket 19 was passing while still
  producing subprocess warnings that may point to leaked helper processes.

  later commands are reruns for noise, cleanup or final confidence rather than
  new implementation.

  directly to stdout.

### INFERRED

- Ticket 19 briefly reached a ready marker, was reviewed almost immediately,
  then rebased after the review and resumed active rework. The review artifact
  names the pre-rebase candidate `357a8c7`, while the branch head at direct
  snapshot was the rebased ready marker `572128d`.
- Ticket 18 is making progress on the live-interactive findings, with unit and
  focused scenario coverage green again, but the broad harness suite was still
  red during the pass and the branch remained dirty after the prior ready
  marker.
- Ticket 21 is still a high-blast-radius worker. It repaired multiple focused
  failures and added executable gate fragments, but the full harness remained
  red and context dropped sharply during the pass.
- The monitor again compressed meaningful state: ticket 19 was simultaneously
  a ready-marker branch, a reviewed candidate, a rebased branch and an active
  dirty rework tree.
- The observer-side `/tmp` failure is another instance of the same local temp
  capacity hazard that has already affected lead/worker gates; even read-only
  observation tooling can hit it if it writes outside the journal.

### DESIGN

- Candidate reviews need a durable relation between the reviewed commit and
  later rebased equivalents, otherwise monitor/status views can show a current
  ready marker whose review belongs to an unreachable pre-rebase commit.
- "Ready marker exists" should not be treated as a terminal worker state while
  the same worker remains running and dirty after review or rebase.
- The first-level monitor should surface current failing gate names and latest
  focused recovery, not just ahead/dirty counts, for workers that are moving
  quickly through red/green loops.
- Observer and lead support tools should avoid `/tmp` by default on this host,
  or preflight temp capacity before even small capture/spool operations.

### INFERRED

- Ticket 19 completed its post-review rework loop and handed off a clean
  replacement candidate. The lead noticed it and began review or acceptance
  work before the observer cutoff, but the outcome was not yet visible.
- Ticket 18 appears to have recovered broad verification after the prior
  focused timing failures, but it remained running and dirty rather than
  producing a replacement marker.

  after transcript compaction. The worker was still preparing the report and
  had not converted staged/unstaged work into commits.

  lead had started a ticket 19 review/acceptance command.

### DESIGN

- Lead-side review and acceptance work should be visible in the shared monitor;
  otherwise a ready ticket can look idle even while the lead is actively
  spending time on it.
- Replacement ready markers should carry their lineage: ticket 19 now has a
  prior reviewed ready marker and a later clean post-review marker.
- Worker context compaction should be timestamped in monitor history because it
  can make context percentages look like a recovery instead of a session reset.
- A ready worker row should include the gate set the worker claims to have run,
  at least as a short durable handoff summary.

### INFERRED

- Ticket 19 completed the full review/accept/land loop during this interval,
  and its landing forced the run's largest process change so far: effort
  records moved off the code integration branch.
- Ticket 21's first marker was short-lived. The lead reviewed it within about
  four minutes and immediately sent a reintegration request, after which the
  worker resumed active dirty rework.

  interactive probe than before, but the lead found that the worker's trust
  override proof still used an insufficient fake/string check. The ticket moved
  back to rework by the cutoff.
- The record-branch coordinator change reduced code-branch churn, but it also
  changed what `ws.py` status reads mid-run; the observer now has to treat the
  cache directory, scratch notes and record-branch-backed monitor as different
  surfaces.

### DESIGN

- Bookkeeping state should have been separate from code state from the start.
  This run needed multiple workarounds and a mid-run coordinator rewrite before
  ticket 19 could land under the stricter record-branch rule.
- A review instruction that asks for a real-runtime proof needs an acceptance
  item with expected public output, not a broad permission. Ticket 18 shows a
  worker can still satisfy itself with a controlled fake unless the real probe
  is required explicitly.
- The lead monitor should expose lead-side review/reintegration sends and their
  pending background receipts. Both ticket 21 and ticket 18 changed state
  because of `ws.py send` commands that were not visible from worker rows
  alone.
- Monitors should preserve disagreement between status sources. Ticket 21's
  dirty count differed between `ws.py status`, `ws.py workers` and direct git
  during the same observer window.

- Worker context should be evented, not just sampled. A compaction/resume count
  beside the context percentage would prevent readers from treating 18% to 91%
  as ordinary progress.
- Gate monitoring needs stable command identifiers with started-at time, suite
  name and latest verdict. Ticket 21's first broad tools run, harness run and
  restarted broad tools run were distinct phases, but the monitor reduced them
  to one prose activity line.
- The protocol needs a first-class "dirty rework validating" state after a
  review/reintegration handoff. Both active tickets were validating changed
  code past a stale ready marker.
- Real interactive proof artifacts should be summarized structurally by the
  worker runtime, including cleanup of proof sessions and run-record paths, so
  the lead does not have to infer proof quality from ad hoc transcript commands.
